03Risk Register and Issue EscalationThe supplier miss is no longer just a possibility+

Your goal

Escalate a confirmed delivery problem while separating the known event, uncertain consequences, and unapproved response.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

The supplier miss is no longer just a possibility

Project manager Rina and procurement lead Omar review a supplier commitment for October 12. The supplier has confirmed that date will be missed and gives October 15 only as an earliest possible date, not a new commitment. Testing planned for October 13 needs the delivery. The risk register still describes a possible delay with a 30% likelihood. No replacement source or recovery plan is approved. Rina must update the status, preserve the history, and request a useful escalation.

Rina: Project manager / Omar: Procurement lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has the supplier confirmed?

Answer and explanation

A. The October 12 commitment will be missed.

The confirmed fact is the original-date miss, while the later date remains only a possibility.

2. What does October 15 represent?

Answer and explanation

D. The earliest possible date mentioned, not a commitment

The briefing explicitly qualifies October 15 as possible rather than committed.

3. Why is the October 13 test exposed?

Answer and explanation

D. It requires the delivery that will miss October 12.

The supplied dependency connects the missed delivery to the planned test start.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The missed commitment is a present issue once confirmed; the replacement date and final effect may still be uncertain. Update the issue and linked risk records without erasing the history or pretending every downstream consequence is already known.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
risk
Uncertainty that may affect a project objective positively or negatively.assess project risk
threat
A potential event or condition with an adverse effect.identify a delivery threat
opportunity
Uncertainty with a potentially beneficial effect on an objective.assess an opportunity
risk register
A record of identified risks, assessment, ownership, and responses.maintain the risk register
issue
A current event, question, or concern requiring management action; in this case, a confirmed delivery problem.log an issue
issue log
A record tracking current problems and their resolution.update the issue log
risk owner
The person accountable for managing a defined risk.assign a risk owner
action owner
The person responsible for carrying out a particular response action.name the action owner
likelihood
The assessed chance of an uncertain event or condition.reassess likelihood
impact
The consequence for a defined objective if an event occurs.assess schedule impact
risk exposure
The project's susceptibility to the relevant uncertainty and consequences.review risk exposure
trigger
A condition or event prompting a defined response.recognize the trigger
risk response
A planned or implemented action addressing an identified risk.develop a risk response
mitigation
Action intended to reduce a threat's likelihood or impact.assess mitigation
contingency plan
A prepared response to be used if specified conditions arise.activate a contingency plan
fallback plan
An alternative response if the primary response does not work.prepare a fallback plan
residual risk
Risk remaining after a response has been applied.monitor residual risk
secondary risk
New risk introduced by a response to another risk.identify secondary risks
escalation threshold
The point at which an issue or risk must be referred to higher authority.apply the escalation threshold
escalation brief
A concise statement of the problem, consequence, and help or decision needed.prepare an escalation brief
decision deadline
The latest time a decision is needed to support the stated plan.state the decision deadline
contingency reserve
Provision for identified uncertainty within the applicable plan.clarify contingency-reserve use
response status
Whether a response is proposed, approved, underway, or completed.report response status
closure evidence
Information showing that the stated closure conditions are met.require closure evidence

Earliest possible October 15 is not promised October 15. A proposed alternative source is not approved mitigation or completed recovery. Separate event status, impact estimate, response status, and the evidence required to close the issue.

C Notice the language

Escalation should explain the need for help

A senior audience needs the confirmed problem, the threatened objective, and the decision or support required. A register entry is not a response plan. Avoid keeping an outdated probability merely because the final delivery date and full project impact are not yet known.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the confirmed event
The supplier has confirmed it will miss October 12.
Correct the old wording
Possible delay no longer describes the commitment status.
Preserve uncertainty
The replacement delivery date is not committed.
Qualify the earliest date
October 15 is the earliest possibility mentioned.
Connect the dependency
The October 13 test requires this delivery.
Keep the history
Link the issue to the original risk entry.
Separate consequences
The delivery miss is confirmed; the full milestone impact is still being assessed.
Name the owner
Assign responsibility for obtaining a committed supplier recovery plan.
Make the request actionable
We need a decision on the feasible response options.
Avoid imaginary recovery
No alternative source has been approved.
Distinguish planning from action
A contingency plan must be checked before it is treated as activated.
Watch new exposure
Expediting can introduce cost or quality risks.
Retain residual risk
A response may reduce exposure without removing every uncertainty.
Set the decision time
State when the decision is needed and why.
Define closure
Close only when the agreed evidence supports closure.
Close the escalation
Report the fact, consequence, owner, response status, and next checkpoint.
6 language and precision notes
Possible
No longer adequate for an event already confirmed.
Earliest
A lower timing bound or possibility, not a promised arrival.
Mitigated
Identify the implemented action and remaining exposure.
Owned
A named person is not evidence that the problem is resolved.
Escalated
Referred for attention, not automatically decided.
Closed
Requires a defined condition and evidence, not a reassuring message.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is accurate now?

Answer and explanation

D. The October 12 miss is confirmed; the replacement date remains uncommitted.

The opening separates the established miss from continuing uncertainty about replacement timing.

2. How should the original risk record be handled?

Answer and explanation

B. Update its status and link it to the current issue while preserving history.

Linked records preserve how the risk developed without leaving an obsolete event status in place.

3. What should an escalation request include?

Answer and explanation

C. The confirmed problem, affected objective, options, decision owner, and needed timing

Decision-useful escalation connects the facts and consequences to specific help or authorization.

4. Which item is a secondary risk of switching to an untested replacement supplier?

Answer and explanation

C. New integration uncertainty introduced by the replacement.

Secondary risk is new uncertainty created by the response. The existing miss is the original issue; an old probability and a record identifier are not new response-created exposures.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

The supplier miss is no longer just a possibility

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rina: Project manager / Omar: Procurement lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • risk register
  • issue
  • escalation brief
  • secondary risks
  • trigger
  • action owner
  • contingency plan
  • closure evidence
  • response status
  • impact
  1. Rina
    The supplier has confirmed it will miss October twelfth, but our register still says possible delivery delay with a thirty-percent likelihood. That wording is now misleading.
  2. Omar
    We have a current 1. The uncertainty is no longer whether the original commitment will be met; it concerns the replacement timing and the consequences for our work.
  3. Rina
    The supplier mentioned October fifteenth as the earliest possible date. I want to make sure nobody turns that into a confirmed recovery in the project update.
  4. Omar
    Keep the 2 explicit. The supplier has not committed to the fifteenth, and we have no approved replacement source or recovery plan.
  5. Rina
    Testing is planned for October thirteenth and requires the delivery. That is the immediate dependency we need to show in the escalation.
  6. Omar
    State the 3 carefully. The planned test is threatened by the confirmed miss, but we still need a supported assessment of the full milestone consequence.
  7. Rina
    Should we remove the original risk entry now? I do not want two records to make it look like two separate supplier problems.
  8. Omar
    Update the 4 and link it to the issue log. Preserve the history and distinguish the event status rather than deleting the warning or duplicating its impact.
  9. Rina
    Our escalation condition was a missed supplier commitment. That has happened, so we should act now rather than wait for a better replacement date.
  10. Omar
    The 5 has occurred. An uncertain recovery date is not a reason to postpone acknowledging the event that activates the response.
  11. Rina
    I need someone clearly responsible for obtaining the supplier's committed plan. A general instruction to procurement will not tell the sponsor who is following up.
  12. Omar
    Name an 6 for that task, with a due time and escalation route. That is separate from pretending the person can guarantee a date the supplier has not committed to.
  13. Rina
    There may be a contingency in the procurement plan, but we should check its conditions and availability before saying it has solved the problem.
  14. Omar
    Exactly. A 7 must be usable under the current facts. A named alternative is not automatically available, qualified, authorized, or fast enough for the test.
  15. Rina
    If we expedite or switch sources, we may create new costs or quality questions. The escalation should not present every response as an uncomplicated improvement.
  16. Omar
    Those are potential 8. Assess them alongside the remaining exposure from the original delay, and keep the response options comparable on a stated basis.
  17. Rina
    I will prepare a short decision request: confirmed miss, affected test, uncommitted replacement date, options under assessment, and the time when support is needed.
  18. Omar
    That is a useful 9. It gives the decision maker something to act on rather than a long register entry with no clear request.
  19. Rina
    What evidence will change the status? I do not want the next reassuring email to close the issue while testing still lacks its required delivery.
  20. Omar
    Require 10. A committed plan may change the status, but completion and closure depend on the agreed conditions and verified result, not the tone of the reply.

Answers and explanations
  1. issue Turn 2. An issue is the confirmed commitment problem that now needs action, even though its final consequences remain uncertain.
  2. response status Turn 4. Response status distinguishes a possible recovery date from an approved or completed response.
  3. impact Turn 6. Impact concerns the effect on the planned test and project objectives, which still needs assessment beyond the confirmed miss.
  4. risk register Turn 8. The risk register should preserve the original risk's history and link it to the current issue.
  5. trigger Turn 10. The trigger is the confirmed supplier miss that prompts the previously identified escalation response.
  6. action owner Turn 12. The action owner is responsible for the specific task of obtaining and reporting the supplier recovery information.
  7. contingency plan Turn 14. A contingency plan is a prepared response whose conditions and readiness must be checked before it is treated as active.
  8. secondary risks Turn 16. Secondary risks are new uncertainties introduced by the response, such as quality or integration exposure from switching suppliers.
  9. escalation brief Turn 18. An escalation brief connects the confirmed problem and consequences to the specific decision or support needed.
  10. closure evidence Turn 20. Closure evidence demonstrates that the defined issue-resolution conditions are met, not merely that a supplier sent a reassuring message.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rina: The supplier has confirmed it will miss October twelfth, but our register still says possible delivery delay with a thirty-percent likelihood. That wording is now misleading.
  2. Omar: We have a current issue. The uncertainty is no longer whether the original commitment will be met; it concerns the replacement timing and the consequences for our work.
  3. Rina: The supplier mentioned October fifteenth as the earliest possible date. I want to make sure nobody turns that into a confirmed recovery in the project update.
  4. Omar: Keep the response status explicit. The supplier has not committed to the fifteenth, and we have no approved replacement source or recovery plan.
  5. Rina: Testing is planned for October thirteenth and requires the delivery. That is the immediate dependency we need to show in the escalation.
  6. Omar: State the impact carefully. The planned test is threatened by the confirmed miss, but we still need a supported assessment of the full milestone consequence.
  7. Rina: Should we remove the original risk entry now? I do not want two records to make it look like two separate supplier problems.
  8. Omar: Update the risk register and link it to the issue log. Preserve the history and distinguish the event status rather than deleting the warning or duplicating its impact.
  9. Rina: Our escalation condition was a missed supplier commitment. That has happened, so we should act now rather than wait for a better replacement date.
  10. Omar: The trigger has occurred. An uncertain recovery date is not a reason to postpone acknowledging the event that activates the response.
  11. Rina: I need someone clearly responsible for obtaining the supplier's committed plan. A general instruction to procurement will not tell the sponsor who is following up.
  12. Omar: Name an action owner for that task, with a due time and escalation route. That is separate from pretending the person can guarantee a date the supplier has not committed to.
  13. Rina: There may be a contingency in the procurement plan, but we should check its conditions and availability before saying it has solved the problem.
  14. Omar: Exactly. A contingency plan must be usable under the current facts. A named alternative is not automatically available, qualified, authorized, or fast enough for the test.
  15. Rina: If we expedite or switch sources, we may create new costs or quality questions. The escalation should not present every response as an uncomplicated improvement.
  16. Omar: Those are potential secondary risks. Assess them alongside the remaining exposure from the original delay, and keep the response options comparable on a stated basis.
  17. Rina: I will prepare a short decision request: confirmed miss, affected test, uncommitted replacement date, options under assessment, and the time when support is needed.
  18. Omar: That is a useful escalation brief. It gives the decision maker something to act on rather than a long register entry with no clear request.
  19. Rina: What evidence will change the status? I do not want the next reassuring email to close the issue while testing still lacks its required delivery.
  20. Omar: Require closure evidence. A committed plan may change the status, but completion and closure depend on the agreed conditions and verified result, not the tone of the reply.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A possible delay becomes an issue

Fictional supplier review using the lesson case: the supplier has confirmed it will miss delivery, but the risk register still says possible delay.

  1. Project analyst: The supplier confirmed a missed delivery date, but our risk register still calls it possible.
  2. Project manager: Then the delay is an issue now. Do we have a confirmed replacement delivery date?
  3. Project analyst: No. Their message confirms the miss but says the revised date is still being assessed.
  4. Project manager: Please create an issue-log entry without turning that unknown date into a planning fact.
  5. Project analyst: Should the original risk entry disappear once the issue has an owner?
  6. Project manager: Keep its history and link it to the issue so we can trace the escalation.
  7. Project analyst: We discussed another supplier earlier. Can I list that as a completed mitigation?
  8. Project manager: No. A discussion is not secured supply. Record it as an option needing assessment.
  9. Project analyst: I'll distinguish the confirmed miss, unknown replacement date, and proposed alternative in today's update.
  10. Project manager: Assign me as owner for coordinating the impact assessment and requesting the revised date.
The permit rumor needs evidence

Fictional venue project: a coordinator hears an unconfirmed rumor that a permit was rejected; the authority has issued no decision.

  1. Venue coordinator: A colleague heard our permit was rejected. Should I escalate a confirmed project blockage?
  2. Project manager: What evidence do we have beyond that conversation? Has the issuing office sent a decision?
  3. Venue coordinator: No decision has arrived. The application tracker still shows that review is in progress.
  4. Project manager: Then rejection is unconfirmed. We can flag uncertainty without reporting a rejection as fact.
  5. Venue coordinator: Would that belong in the risk register rather than the issue log for rejected permits?
  6. Project manager: Yes, describe the possible schedule effect and identify who will verify the application's status.
  7. Venue coordinator: Could reserving an alternative venue be our mitigation, even though it might incur charges?
  8. Project manager: List it as a proposed option requiring cost and approval checks, not an authorized booking.
  9. Venue coordinator: I'll contact the application owner and retain the tracker status as our current evidence.
  10. Project manager: Good. Escalate any confirmed change promptly, with the evidence and affected milestone clearly stated.
A backup exists but has not been tested

Fictional training launch: a risk register says backup laptops mitigate equipment shortages, but the eight stored laptops have not been checked.

  1. Delivery lead: Our equipment risk is marked resolved because eight backup laptops are in storage.
  2. Risk coordinator: Have those laptops been tested with the training application, or have we only counted them?
  3. Delivery lead: Only counted. The storage inventory confirms eight devices, not whether their software is ready.
  4. Risk coordinator: Then their existence is an observation; their readiness is still an assumption behind the mitigation.
  5. Delivery lead: Would reopening the risk imply that every backup laptop is faulty?
  6. Risk coordinator: No. It means the mitigation is unverified, not that failure has been established.
  7. Delivery lead: I'll ask the support lead to own the readiness check. What evidence should we request?
  8. Risk coordinator: Request the check results and any unresolved access requirements for each device, using the project checklist.
  9. Delivery lead: Until those results arrive, I'll report the backup count and the outstanding validation separately.
  10. Risk coordinator: That is clear. We can reassess the remaining risk once the owner confirms actual readiness.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The supplier miss is no longer just a possibility
  1. Read turns 1-10. State October 12 as a confirmed miss and October 15 as an earliest possibility, not a replacement commitment.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Separate the response owner, proposed contingency, and evidence needed for closure.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A confirmed miss, an uncertain replacement

A vendor confirms it cannot deliver Tuesday. Thursday is suggested but not committed. A Wednesday test needs the item. No alternative is approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "Tuesday's miss is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. confirmed

The vendor explicitly states it cannot meet the Tuesday commitment.

2. Buyer: "Thursday remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. uncommitted

The later date is only suggested, with no delivery commitment.

3. Manager: "The Wednesday test has a delivery ___."

Answer and explanation

C. dependency

The test requires the item, linking its timing to the vendor delivery.

4. Buyer: "No alternative has been ___."

Answer and explanation

C. approved

The scenario supplies no authorized substitute or recovery arrangement.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Course: Project Management English

Scope: Original fictional language practice, not a project-management certification, contract interpretation, engineering instruction, or release authorization. Lesson 03: Risk Register and Issue Escalation
Original fictional case: Project manager Rina and procurement lead Omar review a supplier commitment for October 12. The supplier has confirmed that date will be missed and gives October 15 only as an earliest possible date, not a new commitment. Testing planned for October 13 needs the delivery. The risk register still describes a possible delay with a 30% likelihood. No replacement source or recovery plan is approved. Rina must update the status, preserve the history, and request a useful escalation.
Language workshop: The supplier miss is no longer just a possibility
Communication goal: Escalate a confirmed delivery problem while separating the known event, uncertain consequences, and unapproved response.
Language guidance: The missed commitment is a present issue once confirmed; the replacement date and final effect may still be uncertain. Update the issue and linked risk records without erasing the history or pretending every downstream consequence is already known.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The supplier has confirmed it will miss October 12. / Possible delay no longer describes the commitment status. / The replacement delivery date is not committed. / October 15 is the earliest possibility mentioned. / The October 13 test requires this delivery. / Link the issue to the original risk entry. / The delivery miss is confirmed; the full milestone impact is still being assessed. / Assign responsibility for obtaining a committed supplier recovery plan. / We need a decision on the feasible response options. / No alternative source has been approved. / A contingency plan must be checked before it is treated as activated. / Expediting can introduce cost or quality risks. / A response may reduce exposure without removing every uncertainty. / State when the decision is needed and why. / Close only when the agreed evidence supports closure. / Report the fact, consequence, owner, response status, and next checkpoint.
Vocabulary: risk: Uncertainty that may affect a project objective positively or negatively. / threat: A potential event or condition with an adverse effect. / opportunity: Uncertainty with a potentially beneficial effect on an objective. / risk register: A record of identified risks, assessment, ownership, and responses. / issue: A current event, question, or concern requiring management action; in this case, a confirmed delivery problem. / issue log: A record tracking current problems and their resolution. / risk owner: The person accountable for managing a defined risk. / action owner: The person responsible for carrying out a particular response action. / likelihood: The assessed chance of an uncertain event or condition. / impact: The consequence for a defined objective if an event occurs. / risk exposure: The project's susceptibility to the relevant uncertainty and consequences. / trigger: A condition or event prompting a defined response. / risk response: A planned or implemented action addressing an identified risk. / mitigation: Action intended to reduce a threat's likelihood or impact. / contingency plan: A prepared response to be used if specified conditions arise. / fallback plan: An alternative response if the primary response does not work. / residual risk: Risk remaining after a response has been applied. / secondary risk: New risk introduced by a response to another risk. / escalation threshold: The point at which an issue or risk must be referred to higher authority. / escalation brief: A concise statement of the problem, consequence, and help or decision needed. / decision deadline: The latest time a decision is needed to support the stated plan. / contingency reserve: Provision for identified uncertainty within the applicable plan. / response status: Whether a response is proposed, approved, underway, or completed. / closure evidence: Information showing that the stated closure conditions are met.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. State October 12 as a confirmed miss and October 15 as an earliest possibility, not a replacement commitment. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Separate the response owner, proposed contingency, and evidence needed for closure. Complete and check the transfer. Keep Tuesday's miss confirmed, Thursday uncommitted, and Wednesday's test dependent on delivery.
Partner: Rina: Project manager / Omar: Procurement lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A vendor confirms it cannot deliver Tuesday. Thursday is suggested but not committed. A Wednesday test needs the item. No alternative is approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rina: I will prepare a short decision request: confirmed miss, affected test, uncommitted replacement date, options under assessment, and the time when support is needed.
Omar: That is a useful escalation brief. It gives the decision maker something to act on rather than a long register entry with no clear request.
Rina: What evidence will change the status? I do not want the next reassuring email to close the issue while testing still lacks its required delivery.
Omar: Require closure evidence. A committed plan may change the status, but completion and closure depend on the agreed conditions and verified result, not the tone of the reply.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Stakeholder Alignment and GovernanceA recommendation is not an approval+

Your goal

Close a governance meeting with a clear decision status, accountable authority, and accurate record of recommendations and actions.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A recommendation is not an approval

Project manager Rae reviews meeting notes with steering-group chair Simon. The group recommended changing a release feature, but the notes say change approved. The project governance plan reserves this change decision to the sponsor; no written delegation or sponsor decision is recorded. The group can recommend and commission analysis. Rae must correct the minutes, identify the approval route, and separate an action to prepare an assessment from authority to implement the feature change.

Rae: Project manager / Simon: Steering-group chair

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Who holds the stated change authority?

Answer and explanation

B. The sponsor

The governance plan explicitly reserves this change decision to the sponsor.

2. What did the steering group actually do?

Answer and explanation

D. Recommend a change

The brief describes a recommendation and says no sponsor decision or delegation is recorded.

3. What may the group commission under the supplied remit?

Answer and explanation

D. Analysis of the proposal

The group can recommend and request analysis, which is distinct from authorizing implementation.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Consensus in the steering group does not override the sponsor authority stated in this case. A quorum only answers whether the group can validly conduct its own business; it does not enlarge its remit or create an unrecorded delegation.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
governance
The arrangements for project direction, decisions, oversight, and accountability.clarify project governance
steering group
A group guiding the project within its defined authority.convene the steering group
terms of reference
The document defining a group's purpose, membership, and remit.check the terms of reference
remit
The area of responsibility and authority assigned to a person or group.stay within the remit
change authority
The person or body empowered to authorize a specified change.identify the change authority
delegated authority
Decision power explicitly assigned by an authorized person or body.verify delegated authority
approval threshold
A boundary determining the level of authorization required.apply the approval threshold
decision rights
Defined responsibility for making particular decisions.confirm decision rights
recommendation
A proposed course of action submitted for consideration.record a recommendation
endorsement
Expressed support that may not itself constitute formal approval.distinguish endorsement from approval
authorization
Permission from the appropriate authority to take the stated action.obtain authorization
quorum
The minimum participation required for a body's valid proceedings under its rules.confirm a quorum
consensus
Broad agreement among participants on a stated matter.establish the scope of consensus
dissent
A stated disagreement with a proposal or conclusion.record material dissent
decision log
A record of actual decisions, authority, conditions, and dates.maintain the decision log
action log
A record of assigned tasks, owners, deadlines, and status.update the action log
minutes
The formal record of a meeting's relevant proceedings and outcomes.correct the minutes
escalation route
The path for referring a matter beyond the current authority.use the escalation route
stage gate
A defined review point for an authorization or continuation decision.prepare for a stage gate
assurance review
An examination intended to provide confidence in delivery or controls.commission an assurance review
accountability
Answerability for a defined decision or result.assign accountability
RACI
A responsibility model distinguishing responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed roles.clarify the RACI roles
decision paper
A document presenting the issue, options, consequences, and requested decision.prepare a decision paper
conditional approval
Authorization limited by explicit conditions.record conditional approval

An action to prepare analysis is not an instruction to implement the change. Minutes should distinguish recommendation, analysis action, approval request, and actual decision. If an approval is conditional, both the conditions and their status must remain visible.

C Notice the language

Ask the authority question before the room empties

A meeting can reach a strong recommendation without having the authority to implement it. Clarify what was decided, who can authorize the next step, and what the team may do meanwhile. This prevents a cooperative discussion from becoming an ambiguous instruction passed to delivery teams.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Check the record
The minutes say approved, but the group made a recommendation.
Name the authority
The governance plan reserves this decision to the sponsor.
Ask about delegation
Is there an explicit delegation covering this change?
Separate agreement
Consensus here does not by itself provide implementation authority.
Clarify the remit
The group can recommend and commission analysis.
Distinguish the action
Preparing the assessment is not approval to implement.
Frame the request
The sponsor needs the options, impacts, and specific decision sought.
Preserve dissent
Record material objections accurately rather than inventing unanimity.
Correct the status
Mark the change as recommended, with approval pending.
Assign the preparation
Name the owner and deadline for the decision paper.
Keep delivery informed
Tell the team exactly what work is authorized meanwhile.
Avoid a silent decision
No objection in the meeting is not a recorded sponsor approval.
Check a condition
State what must happen before a conditional approval takes effect.
Confirm the outcome
Read back the decision, authority, limits, and action owners.
Keep the records distinct
Put decisions in the decision log and tasks in the action log.
Close the route
Send the recommendation through the defined approval route.
6 language and precision notes
Supported
May mean endorsed, not authorized.
Approved
State by whom, for what, and under which conditions.
Aligned
Clarify whether people agree on the problem, recommendation, or actual action.
Quorate
Valid attendance does not create authority outside the group's remit.
Consulted
Asked for input, not necessarily empowered to approve.
Pending
An open status that must remain visible to implementation teams.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which correction belongs in the minutes?

Answer and explanation

B. Change recommended; sponsor approval pending; analysis commissioned

The correction separates the actual recommendation and permitted analysis from missing approval.

2. The group is quorate and unanimous. What remains necessary before implementing this change?

Answer and explanation

C. Sponsor approval or an explicit applicable delegation; quorum and agreement do not enlarge the stated remit.

Participation rules and support for a recommendation are different from the governance plan's reserved change authority.

3. Which task can proceed under the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

D. Preparing the commissioned impact analysis

Commissioned analysis is within the group's stated remit, unlike the reserved change approval.

4. Which record best captures conditional approval if it is later given?

Answer and explanation

A. The actual approver, permitted change, explicit conditions, and condition status

The conditions define the approval's scope and must not disappear during the handoff.

E Conversations

A recommendation is not an approval

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rae: Project manager / Simon: Steering-group chair

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • delegated authority
  • authorization
  • recommendation
  • conditional approval
  • dissent
  • decision log
  • quorum
  • action log
  • terms of reference
  • decision paper
  1. Simon
    The meeting seemed aligned on changing the feature, and the notes say change approved. I assumed that reflected the room, but you have flagged the wording.
  2. Rae
    The room made a 1. Our governance plan reserves this decision to the sponsor, and the record contains neither a sponsor decision nor an explicit delegation.
  3. Simon
    We had enough members present and everyone supported the proposal. Does that not give the group authority to move the change forward?
  4. Rae
    A 2 lets the group conduct business under its rules. It does not expand the remit or replace a decision explicitly reserved to someone else.
  5. Simon
    Then I should tell the team we support the recommendation, but we have not authorized the build. Can you point me to the decision authority in our plan?
  6. Rae
    Exactly. Check the 3. This group can recommend and commission analysis; it cannot treat its own enthusiasm as permission outside the authority the project has assigned.
  7. Simon
    There may be a delegation somewhere, but I have not seen one. I should not ask the team to proceed on the assumption that it probably exists.
  8. Rae
    Verify 4 before relying on it. Until then, the sponsor remains the stated decision maker for this change, and the implementation status should remain pending.
  9. Simon
    We did assign someone to assess the impact. I want that work to proceed, because the sponsor will need a clearer comparison of the options.
  10. Rae
    Put that in the 5. The task is to prepare analysis with an owner and deadline; it is not an instruction to implement the feature change.
  11. Simon
    What should the sponsor receive? Sending the meeting transcript would show the discussion, but might not make the actual choice very clear.
  12. Rae
    Prepare a 6. Include the options, consequences, recommendation, and specific authorization sought, along with the date when the decision is needed for delivery planning.
  13. Simon
    I will correct the minutes to say recommended rather than approved. Should we also explain that the analysis is authorized while implementation is not?
  14. Rae
    Yes. Keep 7 attached to the specific action. Otherwise the team may hear that work can proceed and assume that means the change itself.
  15. Simon
    Keep the material objections in the paper too. The sponsor needs to see the concerns we discussed, not just a tidy statement that everyone agreed.
  16. Rae
    Record material 8 accurately. The sponsor needs the substantive concerns, not a smoother account that hides the trade-off the group actually discussed.
  17. Simon
    If the sponsor later approves with conditions, the delivery team needs those conditions too. The word approved on its own would still be incomplete.
  18. Rae
    A 9 must state the limits and what must be satisfied. We should not silently treat an unmet condition as though it has already been cleared.
  19. Simon
    I will read back the corrected result: recommendation supported, assessment commissioned, sponsor decision pending. Then we will send the paper through the stated route.
  20. Rae
    Update the 10 accordingly and tell the delivery team the same status. A clear handoff will preserve both useful progress and the actual boundary on implementation.

Answers and explanations
  1. recommendation Turn 2. A recommendation proposes a course of action; it does not establish the reserved sponsor approval.
  2. quorum Turn 4. A quorum satisfies the group's participation rules but does not expand its substantive decision authority.
  3. terms of reference Turn 6. Terms of reference define the steering group's remit, here recommendation and analysis rather than reserved change approval.
  4. delegated authority Turn 8. Delegated authority must be explicitly established; the team cannot infer it from a possible undocumented arrangement.
  5. action log Turn 10. The action log records the authorized assessment task, its owner, and deadline, not a decision to implement.
  6. decision paper Turn 12. A decision paper presents the options, consequences, and specific authorization requested from the proper decision maker.
  7. authorization Turn 14. Authorization identifies exactly which action is permitted; here analysis can proceed while implementation remains unapproved.
  8. dissent Turn 16. Dissent preserves relevant disagreement instead of falsely presenting the recommendation as unanimous or unqualified.
  9. conditional approval Turn 18. Conditional approval permits only the stated action under explicit conditions, whose status must remain visible.
  10. decision log Turn 20. The decision log should reflect actual decisions and their authority, not convert the meeting recommendation into approval.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Simon: The meeting seemed aligned on changing the feature, and the notes say change approved. I assumed that reflected the room, but you have flagged the wording.
  2. Rae: The room made a recommendation. Our governance plan reserves this decision to the sponsor, and the record contains neither a sponsor decision nor an explicit delegation.
  3. Simon: We had enough members present and everyone supported the proposal. Does that not give the group authority to move the change forward?
  4. Rae: A quorum lets the group conduct business under its rules. It does not expand the remit or replace a decision explicitly reserved to someone else.
  5. Simon: Then I should tell the team we support the recommendation, but we have not authorized the build. Can you point me to the decision authority in our plan?
  6. Rae: Exactly. Check the terms of reference. This group can recommend and commission analysis; it cannot treat its own enthusiasm as permission outside the authority the project has assigned.
  7. Simon: There may be a delegation somewhere, but I have not seen one. I should not ask the team to proceed on the assumption that it probably exists.
  8. Rae: Verify delegated authority before relying on it. Until then, the sponsor remains the stated decision maker for this change, and the implementation status should remain pending.
  9. Simon: We did assign someone to assess the impact. I want that work to proceed, because the sponsor will need a clearer comparison of the options.
  10. Rae: Put that in the action log. The task is to prepare analysis with an owner and deadline; it is not an instruction to implement the feature change.
  11. Simon: What should the sponsor receive? Sending the meeting transcript would show the discussion, but might not make the actual choice very clear.
  12. Rae: Prepare a decision paper. Include the options, consequences, recommendation, and specific authorization sought, along with the date when the decision is needed for delivery planning.
  13. Simon: I will correct the minutes to say recommended rather than approved. Should we also explain that the analysis is authorized while implementation is not?
  14. Rae: Yes. Keep authorization attached to the specific action. Otherwise the team may hear that work can proceed and assume that means the change itself.
  15. Simon: Keep the material objections in the paper too. The sponsor needs to see the concerns we discussed, not just a tidy statement that everyone agreed.
  16. Rae: Record material dissent accurately. The sponsor needs the substantive concerns, not a smoother account that hides the trade-off the group actually discussed.
  17. Simon: If the sponsor later approves with conditions, the delivery team needs those conditions too. The word approved on its own would still be incomplete.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Recommendations without an approver

Fictional governance meeting using the lesson case: participants have recommended a change, but approval authority is unclear.

  1. Project manager: We have several recommendations, but who has decision rights for approving this particular change?
  2. Operations lead: I assumed the people in this meeting could approve it because we all supported discussion.
  3. Project manager: Participation doesn't establish approval authority. Can we check the governance arrangements before recording a decision?
  4. Operations lead: The RACI may help, although I don't have the current version in front of me.
  5. Project manager: Let's confirm that document rather than guessing who is accountable for this change category.
  6. Operations lead: Should we send the recommendation straight to the steering committee while we check?
  7. Project manager: We can prepare it, but first invite the delivery team to describe the implementation impact.
  8. Operations lead: They haven't spoken yet. I'll ask them to identify the resource question that remains open.
  9. Project manager: I'll record a recommendation awaiting the confirmed approver, not a change already authorized.
  10. Operations lead: Agreed. We'll close with the open authority question and an owner for resolving it.
Consulted does not mean accountable

Fictional training project: the agreed RACI names the training director as approver and the IT lead as consulted on platform changes.

  1. IT lead: I reviewed the proposed platform change. Why does the meeting note call me the final approver?
  2. Project coordinator: I confused your technical review with approval. Our RACI lists you as consulted, not accountable.
  3. IT lead: Correct. The training director approves, but she needs my compatibility concerns before deciding.
  4. Project coordinator: Could you explain the unresolved concern now so the recommendation includes your actual position?
  5. IT lead: We haven't checked whether existing course links will still work after the change.
  6. Project coordinator: Then your review is not an unconditional endorsement. I'll make that distinction explicit.
  7. IT lead: Please also invite the course administrator, who understands how those links are used daily.
  8. Project coordinator: I'll include that perspective and ask the director whether she needs verification before deciding.
  9. IT lead: Can you confirm that no implementation instruction will be sent on my behalf meanwhile?
  10. Project coordinator: Yes. I'll correct the note and retain the change as awaiting the director's decision.
The absent service desk needs a voice

Fictional steering discussion: leaders favor weekend deployment, but the service desk has not been consulted and support coverage is unknown.

  1. Steering chair: Most participants prefer weekend deployment. Can we close this item as agreed and move on?
  2. Project manager: Before that, the service desk hasn't contributed, and weekend support coverage remains an open question.
  3. Steering chair: Their representative is absent. Does that mean we must abandon today's discussion completely?
  4. Project manager: No. We can record the preference and request their input before you make the final decision.
  5. Steering chair: What specifically should they clarify rather than giving us another general opinion about timing?
  6. Project manager: Whether they can cover the proposed window and what escalation arrangements would be needed.
  7. Steering chair: I hold the decision rights here, but I don't want silence treated as their agreement.
  8. Project manager: I'll mark their view as pending and summarize the operational question for their response.
  9. Steering chair: Please bring back the recommendation once their coverage position is known, including any unresolved gap.
  10. Project manager: Understood. Today's outcome is a preferred option, not authorization to deploy that weekend.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A recommendation is not an approval
  1. Read the governance discussion. Distinguish a quorate meeting and recommendation from the sponsor's reserved change decision.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Read the authorized analysis action without turning it into an implementation instruction.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Authorize analysis, not implementation

A committee may commission analysis but cannot approve a cost change. It recommends an option and requests an assessment. The finance sponsor has not decided.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Chair: "The committee has made a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. recommendation

The committee supports an option without holding the reserved approval authority.

2. Manager: "The authorized next task is the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. assessment

The committee can commission the analysis requested in the briefing.

3. Chair: "The sponsor's decision is still ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pending

The finance sponsor has not made the required decision.

4. Manager: "Implementation is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. authorized

A recommendation and an assessment request do not approve the cost change.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

REFERENCE
Course: Project Management English
Professional audience: project managers, program managers, PMO staff, scrum masters, delivery leads, operations managers, and cross-functional coordinators
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not a project-management certification, contract interpretation, engineering instruction, or release authorization. All organizations, dates, numbers, and decisions are invented. Actual work must follow the governing agreement, approved project controls, applicable rules, and responsible decision makers.
Lesson 04: Stakeholder Alignment and Governance
Original fictional case: Project manager Rae reviews meeting notes with steering-group chair Simon. The group recommended changing a release feature, but the notes say change approved. The project governance plan reserves this change decision to the sponsor; no written delegation or sponsor decision is recorded. The group can recommend and commission analysis. Rae must correct the minutes, identify the approval route, and separate an action to prepare an assessment from authority to implement the feature change.
Language workshop: A recommendation is not an approval
Communication goal: Close a governance meeting with a clear decision status, accountable authority, and accurate record of recommendations and actions.
Language guidance: Consensus in the steering group does not override the sponsor authority stated in this case. A quorum only answers whether the group can validly conduct its own business; it does not enlarge its remit or create an unrecorded delegation.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The minutes say approved, but the group made a recommendation. / The governance plan reserves this decision to the sponsor. / Is there an explicit delegation covering this change? / Consensus here does not by itself provide implementation authority. / The group can recommend and commission analysis. / Preparing the assessment is not approval to implement. / The sponsor needs the options, impacts, and specific decision sought. / Record material objections accurately rather than inventing unanimity. / Mark the change as recommended, with approval pending. / Name the owner and deadline for the decision paper. / Tell the team exactly what work is authorized meanwhile. / No objection in the meeting is not a recorded sponsor approval. / State what must happen before a conditional approval takes effect. / Read back the decision, authority, limits, and action owners. / Put decisions in the decision log and tasks in the action log. / Send the recommendation through the defined approval route.
Vocabulary: governance: The arrangements for project direction, decisions, oversight, and accountability. / steering group: A group guiding the project within its defined authority. / terms of reference: The document defining a group's purpose, membership, and remit. / remit: The area of responsibility and authority assigned to a person or group. / change authority: The person or body empowered to authorize a specified change. / delegated authority: Decision power explicitly assigned by an authorized person or body. / approval threshold: A boundary determining the level of authorization required. / decision rights: Defined responsibility for making particular decisions. / recommendation: A proposed course of action submitted for consideration. / endorsement: Expressed support that may not itself constitute formal approval. / authorization: Permission from the appropriate authority to take the stated action. / quorum: The minimum participation required for a body's valid proceedings under its rules. / consensus: Broad agreement among participants on a stated matter. / dissent: A stated disagreement with a proposal or conclusion. / decision log: A record of actual decisions, authority, conditions, and dates. / action log: A record of assigned tasks, owners, deadlines, and status. / minutes: The formal record of a meeting's relevant proceedings and outcomes. / escalation route: The path for referring a matter beyond the current authority. / stage gate: A defined review point for an authorization or continuation decision. / assurance review: An examination intended to provide confidence in delivery or controls. / accountability: Answerability for a defined decision or result. / RACI: A responsibility model distinguishing responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed roles. / decision paper: A document presenting the issue, options, consequences, and requested decision. / conditional approval: Authorization limited by explicit conditions.
Speaking task: Read the governance discussion. Distinguish a quorate meeting and recommendation from the sponsor's reserved change decision. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Read the authorized analysis action without turning it into an implementation instruction. Complete and check the transfer. State what the committee can commission and what still needs the finance sponsor's decision.
Partner: Rae: Project manager / Simon: Steering-group chair
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A committee may commission analysis but cannot approve a cost change. It recommends an option and requests an assessment. The finance sponsor has not decided.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Simon: If the sponsor later approves with conditions, the delivery team needs those conditions too. The word approved on its own would still be incomplete.
Rae: A conditional approval must state the limits and what must be satisfied. We should not silently treat an unmet condition as though it has already been cleared.
Simon: I will read back the corrected result: recommendation supported, assessment commissioned, sponsor decision pending. Then we will send the paper through the stated route.
Rae: Update the decision log accordingly and tell the delivery team the same status. A clear handoff will preserve both useful progress and the actual boundary on implementation.
END REFERENCE
05Change Requests and Scope CreepJust one more report before launch+

Your goal

Respond constructively to a late scope request by comparing explicit options, effort, schedule effects, and approval status.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Just one more report before launch

Two weeks before launch, sponsor Nadia asks project manager Chen to add a new report. The current plan uses all 20 remaining person-days. The report is estimated at four person-days including testing. The team has assessed two feasible options: defer an existing four-person-day export feature and retain the launch date, or keep all features and extend the schedule by two working days using the planned team. Both need approval. No overtime, extra staff, or reduced testing is assumed.

Nadia: Project sponsor / Chen: Project manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How much effort is estimated for the new report?

Answer and explanation

B. Four person-days including testing

The brief gives a four-person-day estimate and explicitly includes testing.

2. Which assessed option retains the launch date?

Answer and explanation

B. Defer the existing four-person-day export feature

The assessed substitution frees the effort needed for the new report while retaining the date.

3. What is the status of both options?

Answer and explanation

B. They need approval.

The briefing states that neither option has yet received authorization.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Four person-days is effort; two working days is an assessed schedule extension with the planned team. They are not interchangeable units. The supplied options already include testing and assume no extra staff or overtime.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
change request
A recorded proposal to alter agreed project work or parameters.raise a change request
change control
The process for assessing and deciding changes to an approved reference.follow change control
scope creep
Uncontrolled growth in project work without corresponding assessment and authorization.prevent scope creep
change log
A record of proposed and decided project changes.update the change log
impact assessment
Analysis of how a proposal affects relevant project dimensions.complete an impact assessment
effort estimate
The amount of work expected, expressed in person-time or another work unit.qualify the effort estimate
person-day
One person's defined day of work used as an effort unit.estimate effort in person-days
elapsed duration
The time passing from an activity's start to finish.distinguish elapsed duration
capacity limit
The amount of work the stated resources can support.explain the capacity limit
trade-off
A choice between competing benefits, costs, or constraints.present the trade-off
scope substitution
Replacing one part of planned work with another under an authorized change.assess a scope substitution
deferred feature
A capability moved out of the current delivery scope or timing.identify the deferred feature
incremental cost
Additional cost caused by the proposed change.assess incremental cost
opportunity cost
Value forgone by choosing one use of resources over another.explain the opportunity cost
benefit impact
The effect of a change on the value expected from the project.assess benefit impact
rework
Work repeated or revised because of a change or defect.estimate rework
test coverage
The extent to which specified requirements or conditions are tested.preserve required test coverage
release boundary
The defined set of capabilities included in a particular release.clarify the release boundary
change freeze
A point or period limiting changes under the project's stated rules.apply the change-freeze rules
baseline revision
An authorized update to the controlled project reference.record a baseline revision
approval record
Evidence of the actual authorization and its limits.retain the approval record
implementation instruction
A clear direction specifying the authorized change to perform.issue an implementation instruction
option comparison
An assessment of alternatives on a consistent basis.prepare an option comparison
decision latency
Time between needing a decision and receiving it.account for decision latency

Deferring the export preserves the date but changes the delivered capability and possible benefit. Keeping all features preserves scope but moves timing. Neither is a free addition, and an authorized change is not the same as uncontrolled scope creep.

C Notice the language

A constructive boundary includes options

A sponsor may hear an immediate no as resistance, but an unqualified yes can conceal a delivery problem. Acknowledge the value, explain the capacity limit, and present the assessed options. The decision is about priorities and consequences, not whether the team is sufficiently willing.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the value
I understand why the new report would be useful at launch.
State the current limit
The plan already uses all 20 remaining person-days.
Quantify the addition
The report is estimated at four person-days including testing.
Avoid unit confusion
Effort is four person-days; the assessed extension is two working days.
Present the substitution
We can retain the date by deferring the existing export feature.
Present the timing option
We can keep all features with the assessed two-working-day extension.
Name the lost capability
The substitution means the export is not in this release.
Keep assumptions visible
Neither option assumes extra staff, overtime, or reduced testing.
Protect the decision
We need approval before changing the committed scope or date.
Separate value and capacity
A valuable request still consumes delivery effort.
Compare consequences
Review the timing and benefit effects on the same basis.
Avoid a hidden shortcut
Removing required testing is not one of the assessed options.
Record the request
Put the new report and its impacts in the change record.
Check decision timing
A late decision may require the estimates to be refreshed.
Preserve the baseline
Update the controlled plan only after the actual decision.
Close the choice
Confirm which option is approved and what the team should implement.
6 language and precision notes
Small
A description that does not replace an effort estimate.
Just
Can conceal additional work when used before a new requirement.
Can
State the conditions under which delivery is feasible.
Swap
Still changes scope and may change expected value.
Estimate
A supported forecast, not an unconditional guarantee.
Approved
Identify the selected option, limits, and implementation date.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which option summary preserves the assessed trade-offs?

Answer and explanation

D. Defer the four-person-day export to retain the date, or keep all features with the assessed two-working-day extension; approval is pending.

Equal effort does not mean equal capability, and effort differs from elapsed duration. Each assessed option changes either delivered scope or timing and still needs authorization.

2. Why does the four-person-day estimate not mean a four-day extension here?

Answer and explanation

B. Effort and elapsed duration differ; the assessed team plan gives two working days.

The supplied assessment distinguishes total work from elapsed schedule time with the planned resources.

3. What must the date-preserving option disclose?

Answer and explanation

D. The existing export will be deferred from this release.

The option preserves time by changing scope, so the removed capability must remain explicit.

4. What turns an addition into uncontrolled scope creep?

Answer and explanation

A. Implementing extra work without the required assessment and authorization

Controlled changes can be legitimate; uncontrolled expansion bypasses the project's stated decision process.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

Just one more report before launch

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nadia: Project sponsor / Chen: Project manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • elapsed duration
  • decision latency
  • scope substitution
  • benefit impact
  • test coverage
  • effort estimate
  • change request
  • option comparison
  • approval record
  • baseline revision
  1. Nadia
    Could we add one more report before launch? It would be useful for the operations review, and I hope it is a small enough change to fit.
  2. Chen
    Let us record the 1. The current plan already uses all twenty remaining person-days, so the report cannot be treated as an addition with no consequence.
  3. Nadia
    I understand it needs work. What does the estimate include? I do not want a build estimate that leaves testing to become a surprise later.
  4. Chen
    The 2 is four person-days including testing. The team has assessed two options using the planned people, with no extra staff or overtime assumed.
  5. Nadia
    Please explain those options in terms of the launch and what users will receive. That will help me compare the value instead of debating whether four is a big number.
  6. Chen
    The first is a 3. Defer the existing four-person-day export feature and use that capacity for the new report, retaining the launch date.
  7. Nadia
    The date stays, but users lose the export from this release. Please put that beside the new report so nobody reads this as an addition with no loss.
  8. Chen
    Exactly. Assess the 4 of deferring the export. A date-preserving option still changes the release content and may affect the work users can do.
  9. Nadia
    What is the second option if the export remains important enough that we should not remove it from this release?
  10. Chen
    Keep all features and accept the assessed 5 increase of two working days. That is the team's schedule assessment, not a claim that four person-days always equals two days.
  11. Nadia
    Both options include the same testing expectation, correct? I would not want an apparently unchanged date achieved by quietly leaving out required checks.
  12. Chen
    Correct. We have not reduced 6 to create either option. If someone proposes a different approach, it needs its own assessment rather than being smuggled into the estimate.
  13. Nadia
    We should compare the two options together: date retained with export deferred, or all features retained with a later launch. Neither means no trade-off.
  14. Chen
    That is the 7. Include the assumptions and any relevant consequences so the decision is about the actual alternatives, not a more attractive label.
  15. Nadia
    I am not choosing an option in this conversation yet. I need to confirm the export's importance with the operations lead before making the decision.
  16. Chen
    Then the 8 should remain pending. The delivery team should not infer an instruction to remove the export or move the date from our discussion of possibilities.
  17. Nadia
    How long is this comparison valid? If the team starts the export before I decide, I assume we need to revisit the estimate rather than reuse it unchanged.
  18. Chen
    We must account for 9. Confirm a decision checkpoint with the team; if the work has moved on, refresh the assessment rather than reuse an estimate whose assumptions no longer hold.
  19. Nadia
    Once the decision is made, we should tell stakeholders exactly what changed. I do not want one audience expecting the original export and another expecting the new report.
  20. Chen
    Issue the authorized 10 and implementation instruction together. That gives the team and stakeholders one supported version of the release instead of several incompatible promises.

Answers and explanations
  1. change request Turn 2. A change request captures the proposed new report so its effort, impacts, and approval can be assessed.
  2. effort estimate Turn 4. The effort estimate is four person-days and explicitly includes testing rather than counting only construction.
  3. scope substitution Turn 6. Scope substitution replaces the existing four-person-day export feature with the new report under an approved change.
  4. benefit impact Turn 8. Benefit impact concerns the value affected when the planned export is deferred, even though the launch date is retained.
  5. elapsed duration Turn 10. Elapsed duration is the schedule time; this option extends it by two working days, distinct from four person-days of effort.
  6. test coverage Turn 12. Test coverage remains within the assessed work; silently dropping required checks is not one of the options.
  7. option comparison Turn 14. The option comparison presents both alternatives on a common scope, time, and assumption basis.
  8. approval record Turn 16. The approval record must reflect the actual decision; no option has been authorized in this conversation.
  9. decision latency Turn 18. Decision latency is the time taken to receive the needed choice, which can change the feasibility of the assessed options.
  10. baseline revision Turn 20. A baseline revision updates the controlled plan after approval and communicates the selected scope and timing.
Read the complete conversation
