Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A late report needs a tradeoff

Fictional launch negotiation using the lesson case: a new report is requested two weeks before launch; it requires moving another feature or extending the schedule.

  1. Sponsor: Can you add the new report before launch in two weeks? It would help our review.
  2. Project manager: We can assess that change request, but the team cannot add it within the unchanged baseline.
  3. Sponsor: What choices would let us include the report without simply asking everyone to work longer?
  4. Project manager: We could move another feature out of this release or propose a later launch date.
  5. Sponsor: I'd rather move a feature. Can you choose something small and tell the team immediately?
  6. Project manager: First we need impact analysis and confirmation that the displaced feature's stakeholders accept that proposal.
  7. Sponsor: Then show me the affected work and the decisions required, not just a revised feature list.
  8. Project manager: Agreed. Until approval, the existing scope and schedule remain our baseline for reporting.
  9. Sponsor: I'll review the options and speak with the stakeholder whose feature might move.
  10. Project manager: I'll bring the tradeoffs for that discussion without treating today's request as authorization.
Five small additions make real work

Fictional portal project: five separate wording and layout requests arrived after scope approval; their combined effort has not been assessed.

  1. Customer representative: These are only five small improvements. Can the designers absorb them without a formal change discussion?
  2. Project manager: Perhaps some are small individually, but we haven't assessed their combined effort or testing impact.
  3. Customer representative: I don't want paperwork to delay fixing labels that users genuinely find confusing.
  4. Project manager: Neither do I. Let's review them together and distinguish essential corrections from new behavior.
  5. Customer representative: One request adds a filter, while the other four change text or positioning.
  6. Project manager: That filter may affect scope differently. Could we prioritize the requests before estimating a package?
  7. Customer representative: Yes. The unclear labels come first; the filter could wait if it threatens delivery.
  8. Project manager: I'll arrange the assessment against our baseline and bring a proposed split for approval.
  9. Customer representative: Please don't promise all five simply because I called them small at the start.
  10. Project manager: Understood. We'll avoid scope creep by making the accepted work and deferred work explicit.
Replacing a workshop with recordings

Fictional onboarding project: the approved scope includes two live workshops; a sponsor requests ten edited recordings instead.

  1. Sponsor: Could we replace the two live workshops with ten recordings and keep the same delivery date?
  2. Project manager: That changes the deliverables, not just the format. Editing and review effort need an impact analysis.
  3. Sponsor: But removing live delivery should free time. Why wouldn't the effort simply balance out?
  4. Project manager: It may free facilitator time, but recording production uses different work and possibly different people.
  5. Sponsor: Could we start with two unedited demonstrations while you assess the full recording proposal?
  6. Project manager: We can examine that option, provided we clarify quality expectations and who must approve them.
  7. Sponsor: I can explain the intended audience, but I cannot authorize production resources from another department.
  8. Project manager: Then I'll take the alternative to that resource owner before revising the project baseline.
  9. Sponsor: Please keep the original workshops in place until the replacement is actually approved.
  10. Project manager: Agreed. We'll compare the options and confirm the replacement decision rather than silently changing scope.

  2. Switch roles. Present the export substitution and schedule extension, keeping testing and approval conditions unchanged.

A substitution changes scope

A new feature needs three person-days. The assessed date-preserving option removes another three-person-day feature. Both cannot fit under the unchanged plan, and no option is approved.

Answer and explanation

The date-preserving option removes that feature from the current delivery.

Vocabulary: change request: A recorded proposal to alter agreed project work or parameters. / change control: The process for assessing and deciding changes to an approved reference. / scope creep: Uncontrolled growth in project work without corresponding assessment and authorization. / change log: A record of proposed and decided project changes. / impact assessment: Analysis of how a proposal affects relevant project dimensions. / effort estimate: The amount of work expected, expressed in person-time or another work unit. / person-day: One person's defined day of work used as an effort unit. / elapsed duration: The time passing from an activity's start to finish. / capacity limit: The amount of work the stated resources can support. / trade-off: A choice between competing benefits, costs, or constraints. / scope substitution: Replacing one part of planned work with another under an authorized change. / deferred feature: A capability moved out of the current delivery scope or timing. / incremental cost: Additional cost caused by the proposed change. / opportunity cost: Value forgone by choosing one use of resources over another. / benefit impact: The effect of a change on the value expected from the project. / rework: Work repeated or revised because of a change or defect. / test coverage: The extent to which specified requirements or conditions are tested. / release boundary: The defined set of capabilities included in a particular release. / change freeze: A point or period limiting changes under the project's stated rules. / baseline revision: An authorized update to the controlled project reference. / approval record: Evidence of the actual authorization and its limits. / implementation instruction: A clear direction specifying the authorized change to perform. / option comparison: An assessment of alternatives on a consistent basis. / decision latency: Time between needing a decision and receiving it.
Speaking task: Read the change discussion. Say four person-days of effort and two working days of assessed extension as different quantities. Switch roles. Present the export substitution and schedule extension, keeping testing and approval conditions unchanged. Complete and check the transfer. Keep the three-person-day substitution as a scope change that has not yet been approved.

Optional second-round challenge: A new feature needs three person-days. The assessed date-preserving option removes another three-person-day feature. Both cannot fit under the unchanged plan, and no option is approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nadia: How long is this comparison valid? If the team starts the export before I decide, I assume we need to revisit the estimate rather than reuse it unchanged.
Chen: We must account for decision latency. Confirm a decision checkpoint with the team; if the work has moved on, refresh the assessment rather than reuse an estimate whose assumptions no longer hold.
Nadia: Once the decision is made, we should tell stakeholders exactly what changed. I do not want one audience expecting the original export and another expecting the new report.
Chen: Issue the authorized baseline revision and implementation instruction together. That gives the team and stakeholders one supported version of the release instead of several incompatible promises.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Status Reporting and Executive UpdatesEight tasks finished, one date still threatened+

Your goal

Produce a concise executive update that shows material threats, rating criteria, and decision needs without confusing task counts with delivery confidence.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Eight tasks finished, one date still threatened

Project lead Sofia and sponsor James review a green report. Eight of ten tasks are complete, but the remaining access approval is needed by October 9 for an October 12 rehearsal before the planned October 16 launch. Approval is unconfirmed. The fictional reporting rules require amber when a material threat needs management attention and green only when delivery is on track without such a threat. No effort weights, cost data, approved recovery, or confirmed launch delay are supplied.

Sofia: Project lead / James: Project sponsor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does eight of ten establish?

Answer and explanation

B. Eighty percent of the task count is complete.

The brief supplies an unweighted task count, not effort weighting or complete delivery evidence.

2. Why does the access approval matter?

Answer and explanation

C. It is needed for the rehearsal that precedes launch.

The stated chain links access due October 9 to rehearsal and the planned launch.

3. Which rating matches the supplied rules and facts?

Answer and explanation

C. Amber

The rules require amber for a material threat needing management attention, which the open dependency creates.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Eight of ten means 80% of task count, not necessarily 80% of effort, value, or readiness. Unequal task sizes and a controlling dependency make that distinction important. No earned-value or cost-performance result can be calculated from these counts alone.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
status report
A periodic account of progress, outlook, problems, and decisions needed.prepare a status report
status date
The date to which the reported information applies.state the status date
reporting period
The time interval covered by a report.define the reporting period
RAG rating
A red-amber-green status classification under stated criteria.apply the RAG rating
green status
An on-track classification under the project's defined reporting rules.justify green status
amber status
A warning classification requiring attention under the defined rules.explain amber status
red status
A serious-problem classification under the defined reporting rules.explain red status
delivery confidence
An assessment of the likelihood of achieving the intended delivery outcome.assess delivery confidence
key milestone
A major event used to judge project progress or readiness.report a key milestone
dependency owner
The person accountable for managing a required external or linked input.identify the dependency owner
dependency due date
The date a required input must be available for the plan.confirm the dependency due date
leading indicator
A signal of a possible future result or problem.monitor a leading indicator
lagging indicator
A measure of an outcome that has already occurred.distinguish a lagging indicator
exception report
A focused report on a deviation or condition requiring attention.raise an exception report
percent complete
Completion expressed as a percentage under a specified measurement basis.define percent complete
task-count completion
The share of listed tasks marked complete without weighting their effort.qualify task-count completion
weighted progress
Progress calculated using stated effort, value, or other weights.report weighted progress
earned value
The budgeted value of work actually completed under a defined measurement method.distinguish earned value
planned value
The budgeted value of work planned by the status date.compare with planned value
actual cost
Cost incurred for the work under the stated accounting basis.report actual cost
cost performance index
Earned value divided by actual cost, commonly CPI.interpret the cost performance index
schedule performance index
Earned value divided by planned value, commonly SPI.interpret the schedule performance index
confidence qualifier
Wording that states a forecast's uncertainty or conditions.add a confidence qualifier
executive ask
The specific decision or support requested from senior management.state the executive ask

Under these fictional rules, the open material threat warrants amber even before a confirmed launch delay. That does not prove October 16 is impossible. Report the planned date, uncertainty, dependency deadline, and required attention without inventing either certainty or failure.

C Notice the language

A color should invite the right conversation

Use the agreed reporting rules rather than choosing the color that sounds most reassuring. A material threat can require attention before a date is actually missed. A concise update should identify the dependency, consequence, owner, and needed action, not hide the problem behind a long list of completed work.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the exception
The access dependency threatens the planned launch sequence.
State the rating basis
Our rules require amber for a material threat needing management attention.
Keep progress visible
Eight of ten listed tasks are complete.
Qualify the percentage
That is task-count completion, not weighted effort.
Name the missing input
The access approval is still unconfirmed.
Give the needed-by date
We need that approval by October 9.
Connect the milestone
Rehearsal is planned for October 12 before the October 16 launch.
Avoid unsupported certainty
The launch is not yet confirmed delayed, but the threat is material.
Avoid a false green
Completed tasks do not remove the remaining dependency.
Name the owner
Identify who is responsible for obtaining the approval.
State the executive ask
We need help securing a timely decision through the authorized route.
Keep the baseline visible
The planned launch remains October 16 pending further evidence or approval.
Separate data gaps
These task counts do not provide earned-value or cost information.
Set the checkpoint
Confirm when the approval status will next be reviewed.
Report change honestly
Update the outlook when evidence changes, not when a color becomes uncomfortable.
Close in one sequence
Rating, dependency, impact, owner, needed action, next update.
6 language and precision notes
Green
Apply the actual criteria, not the mood of the meeting.
Complete
Complete under which definition and measurement method?
Eighty percent
State the denominator and any weighting.
Threatened
A material uncertainty, not necessarily a confirmed delay.
On track
Needs a supported outlook for the remaining work.
Escalation
A request for attention or decision, not automatic authorization.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which executive summary is best supported?

Answer and explanation

A. Amber: access due October 9 is unconfirmed; rehearsal and launch are exposed; owner and decision follow-up needed.

This summary follows the stated criteria and names the material dependency without inventing a final delay.

2. Why is "80% of the project is done" too broad?

Answer and explanation

D. Only unweighted task counts are supplied.

Task-count completion does not establish weighted effort, value, or the importance of remaining work.

3. A colleague divides eight completed tasks by ten total tasks and labels 0.8 as CPI. What is missing?

Answer and explanation

C. Earned value and actual cost under a defined measurement basis.

CPI compares earned value with actual cost. Task counts supply neither quantity and do not measure the cost efficiency of completed work.

4. Which wording preserves uncertainty accurately?

Answer and explanation

B. October 16 remains planned, but the unconfirmed dependency creates a material threat.

The wording distinguishes the planned date from confidence in meeting it.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

Eight tasks finished, one date still threatened

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sofia: Project lead / James: Project sponsor

  12. Sofia
    That is the appropriate 6. We need support securing a timely decision through the proper route, not an assurance that an unapproved input can be treated as available.

    The report also uses the words value delivered beside the eighty-percent figure. We do not have an earned-value calculation here, do we?

    No. 7 needs its defined work and budget basis. The task count supplies neither that measure nor the actual cost needed for a cost-performance calculation.

    Remove the unsupported value label. Give me progress, the access threat, the needed decision, and the deadline in one short update for the sponsor group.
  16. Sofia

    I agree that amber follows our rules. It is not a punishment for the team; it tells us where management attention can still make a difference.
  18. Sofia
    Correct. 9 depends on the remaining work and dependencies, not just historical completion. The rating should change when the relevant evidence changes.

Answers and explanations
  1. RAG rating Turn 2. The RAG rating must follow the stated criteria, including material threats, rather than simply count completed tasks.
  2. task-count completion Turn 4. Task-count completion is eight finished tasks out of ten, without weighting their size or importance.
  3. dependency due date Turn 6. The dependency due date is October 9, when approval is needed to support the planned rehearsal.
  4. confidence qualifier Turn 8. A confidence qualifier distinguishes the planned date from the uncertainty created by the unresolved dependency.
  5. dependency owner Turn 10. The dependency owner is accountable for managing the required access input and its follow-up.
  6. executive ask Turn 12. The executive ask requests support for a timely authorized decision, not permission to bypass the required control.
  7. Earned value Turn 14. Earned value measures budgeted completed work under a defined method; unweighted task counts do not provide it.
  8. exception report Turn 16. An exception report focuses attention on the condition needing action while preserving its relevant progress context.
  9. Delivery confidence Turn 18. Delivery confidence concerns the remaining path to the intended outcome, not merely how many historical tasks are complete.
  10. status date Turn 20. The status date identifies when the report's facts and outlook apply, making later changes traceable.
Read the complete conversation
  1. James: The report is green and shows eight of ten tasks complete. That sounds encouraging, but I see the access approval is still open. Does it affect the rehearsal?
  2. Sofia: Yes. Our RAG rating should reflect that dependency. The supplied rules require amber when a material threat needs management attention, even before a milestone is confirmed late.
  3. James: Keep the eight completed tasks in the update, but put the access problem first. Which unfinished task is driving the threat to rehearsal?
  4. Sofia: Exactly. That is task-count completion, not eighty percent of all effort or readiness. The remaining tasks may differ in size, and one contains the input needed for rehearsal.
  5. James: Please state the dates in the short update. I need to see the point at which the missing approval affects the rest of the plan.
  6. Sofia: The dependency due date is October ninth. Rehearsal is planned for October twelfth, ahead of the October sixteenth launch, so the approval cannot remain an unrelated footnote.
  7. James: We do not yet know that launch will be late, though. I want the report to show the threat without declaring a final outcome the evidence does not establish.

  9. James: Who is responsible for obtaining the approval? A clear owner would help me understand where support is needed instead of sending a general chase to everyone.
  10. Sofia: Identify the dependency owner and the authorized decision route. The report should say who is following up, what decision is required, and when the next status check will occur.
  11. James: I can help bring the decision to the right forum, but I cannot treat the urgency as permission to bypass the access approval itself.
  12. Sofia: That is the appropriate executive ask. We need support securing a timely decision through the proper route, not an assurance that an unapproved input can be treated as available.
  13. James: The report also uses the words value delivered beside the eighty-percent figure. We do not have an earned-value calculation here, do we?
  14. Sofia: No. Earned value needs its defined work and budget basis. The task count supplies neither that measure nor the actual cost needed for a cost-performance calculation.
  15. James: Remove the unsupported value label. Give me progress, the access threat, the needed decision, and the deadline in one short update for the sponsor group.
  16. Sofia: An exception report can make that concise. It should expose the access issue without forcing the sponsor to discover it inside a long list of activities that are already finished.
  17. James: I agree that amber follows our rules. It is not a punishment for the team; it tells us where management attention can still make a difference.
  18. Sofia: Correct. Delivery confidence depends on the remaining work and dependencies, not just historical completion. The rating should change when the relevant evidence changes.
  19. James: Please correct the report and confirm the next checkpoint. Keep the October sixteenth baseline visible while distinguishing it from any later forecast we may need.
  20. Sofia: I will state the status date and current approval position, change the rating to amber, and include the owner and decision follow-up. That gives the next audience the same accurate picture.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Green conceals the unresolved dependency

Fictional status review using the lesson case: an unresolved dependency threatens the date, and project reporting criteria require material threats to be visible.

  1. Reporting analyst: The project is still green, although the unresolved dependency threatens the planned completion date.
  2. Project manager: Then the report isn't showing the material threat required by our reporting criteria.
  3. Reporting analyst: Most tasks are complete. Could I keep the green RAG status and mention the dependency below?
  4. Project manager: Task completion alone doesn't establish schedule confidence. Apply the agreed criteria to the threat itself.
  5. Reporting analyst: Should I state that the date has already slipped, or would that overstate the evidence?
  6. Project manager: It would overstate it. Say the date is threatened and explain which dependency remains unresolved.
  7. Reporting analyst: What decision ask would make this useful for the executive meeting rather than merely alarming?
  8. Project manager: Ask the dependency owner to confirm their delivery position so we can assess the forecast.
  9. Reporting analyst: I'll separate completed tasks, pending input, and the unconfirmed effect on the date.
  10. Project manager: Good. Show the reason for the status and avoid assigning a color without context.
Scanned is not accepted

Fictional digitization project: 80 of 100 batches are scanned, but 12 scanned batches await quality review and acceptance totals are unknown.

  1. Executive sponsor: Eighty batches scanned sounds like eighty percent delivered. Can I use that in tomorrow's update?
  2. Project manager: You can say eighty percent scanned, but accepted delivery is a different measure in this project.
  3. Executive sponsor: What remains between scanning and acceptance that changes the interpretation of that progress figure?
  4. Project manager: Twelve scanned batches await quality review. We haven't confirmed the overall accepted-batch count yet.
  5. Executive sponsor: The scanned count does not establish the accepted count. Have I understood that distinction correctly?
  6. Project manager: Correct. Our status report should show scanning and acceptance separately, with pending quality reviews visible.
  7. Executive sponsor: Please keep the update brief. What can you state now without waiting for every detail?
  8. Project manager: Eighty batches are scanned; twelve await review; the verified acceptance total is being reconciled.
  9. Executive sponsor: That's clear. Ask the quality owner to supply the acceptance count before we claim delivery progress.
  10. Project manager: I'll do that and label any incomplete figure instead of presenting it as verified.
An executive update with a real ask

Fictional records project: the migration rehearsal passed, but the records owner has not selected which archive to retire.

  1. Project manager: The migration rehearsal passed. Retirement planning is pending because the records owner hasn't selected the archive.
  2. Portfolio director: Your status report lists that as a concern, but what exactly do you need from me?
  3. Project manager: Please help secure a decision meeting with the records owner; I cannot choose their retention approach.
  4. Portfolio director: Does this mean the technical migration has failed, or only that the business decision remains open?
  5. Project manager: The rehearsal passed under its test conditions. The open decision blocks planning the archive retirement.
  6. Portfolio director: Can you explain that distinction in one sentence during the executive update?
  7. Project manager: Technical rehearsal is complete, but retirement planning awaits the records owner's choice of archive.
  8. Portfolio director: Good. Include the options they must consider, without recommending deletion before their review.
  9. Project manager: I'll state the decision ask and the pending dependency alongside the completed rehearsal.
  10. Portfolio director: I'll arrange the discussion and confirm who needs to attend before we set expectations.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Eight tasks finished, one date still threatened
  1. Read the executive update. Stress eight of ten tasks, access needed October 9, rehearsal October 12, and launch planned October 16.
  2. Switch roles. Apply the supplied amber rule without asserting either guaranteed launch or a confirmed delay.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Counted progress does not remove a blocker

Six of eight tasks are complete. A material dependency remains open. The local rules require amber for that threat, and no final delay is confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lead: "Task-count completion is ___ percent."

Answer and explanation

B. seventy-five

Six divided by eight equals seventy-five percent of the listed task count.

2. Sponsor: "That does not establish weighted ___."

Answer and explanation

A. progress

No effort or value weights are supplied for the eight tasks.

3. Lead: "Under our rules, the rating is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. amber

The stated local rules assign amber to the material unresolved dependency.

4. Sponsor: "A final delay is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. confirmed

The briefing identifies a threat without establishing a final delay.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 06: Status Reporting and Executive Updates
Original fictional case: Project lead Sofia and sponsor James review a green report. Eight of ten tasks are complete, but the remaining access approval is needed by October 9 for an October 12 rehearsal before the planned October 16 launch. Approval is unconfirmed. The fictional reporting rules require amber when a material threat needs management attention and green only when delivery is on track without such a threat. No effort weights, cost data, approved recovery, or confirmed launch delay are supplied.
Language workshop: Eight tasks finished, one date still threatened
Communication goal: Produce a concise executive update that shows material threats, rating criteria, and decision needs without confusing task counts with delivery confidence.
Language guidance: Eight of ten means 80% of task count, not necessarily 80% of effort, value, or readiness. Unequal task sizes and a controlling dependency make that distinction important. No earned-value or cost-performance result can be calculated from these counts alone.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The access dependency threatens the planned launch sequence. / Our rules require amber for a material threat needing management attention. / Eight of ten listed tasks are complete. / That is task-count completion, not weighted effort. / The access approval is still unconfirmed. / We need that approval by October 9. / Rehearsal is planned for October 12 before the October 16 launch. / The launch is not yet confirmed delayed, but the threat is material. / Completed tasks do not remove the remaining dependency. / Identify who is responsible for obtaining the approval. / We need help securing a timely decision through the authorized route. / The planned launch remains October 16 pending further evidence or approval. / These task counts do not provide earned-value or cost information. / Confirm when the approval status will next be reviewed. / Update the outlook when evidence changes, not when a color becomes uncomfortable. / Rating, dependency, impact, owner, needed action, next update.
Vocabulary: status report: A periodic account of progress, outlook, problems, and decisions needed. / status date: The date to which the reported information applies. / reporting period: The time interval covered by a report. / RAG rating: A red-amber-green status classification under stated criteria. / green status: An on-track classification under the project's defined reporting rules. / amber status: A warning classification requiring attention under the defined rules. / red status: A serious-problem classification under the defined reporting rules. / delivery confidence: An assessment of the likelihood of achieving the intended delivery outcome. / key milestone: A major event used to judge project progress or readiness. / dependency owner: The person accountable for managing a required external or linked input. / dependency due date: The date a required input must be available for the plan. / leading indicator: A signal of a possible future result or problem. / lagging indicator: A measure of an outcome that has already occurred. / exception report: A focused report on a deviation or condition requiring attention. / percent complete: Completion expressed as a percentage under a specified measurement basis. / task-count completion: The share of listed tasks marked complete without weighting their effort. / weighted progress: Progress calculated using stated effort, value, or other weights. / earned value: The budgeted value of work actually completed under a defined measurement method. / planned value: The budgeted value of work planned by the status date. / actual cost: Cost incurred for the work under the stated accounting basis. / cost performance index: Earned value divided by actual cost, commonly CPI. / schedule performance index: Earned value divided by planned value, commonly SPI. / confidence qualifier: Wording that states a forecast's uncertainty or conditions. / executive ask: The specific decision or support requested from senior management.
Speaking task: Read the executive update. Stress eight of ten tasks, access needed October 9, rehearsal October 12, and launch planned October 16. Switch roles. Apply the supplied amber rule without asserting either guaranteed launch or a confirmed delay. Complete and check the transfer. Say 75% of task count, not weighted progress, and retain the material open dependency.
Partner: Sofia: Project lead / James: Project sponsor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Six of eight tasks are complete. A material dependency remains open. The local rules require amber for that threat, and no final delay is confirmed.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Vendor and Cross-Functional DeliveryComplete on the vendor side, not yet accepted+

Your goal

Resolve a delivery-status disagreement by linking agreed criteria to evidence, distinguishing missing proof from failure, and naming the acceptance authority.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Complete on the vendor side, not yet accepted

Vendor lead Marta says a workflow deliverable is complete. Client acceptance lead Leo can verify four of six agreed criteria, but finds no evidence for C5, audit-log export, or C6, role-based access behavior. The missing evidence does not itself prove those functions fail. No acceptance or waiver is recorded, and Leo cannot waive criteria alone. Contractual payment consequences have not been assessed. Leo and Marta must agree the evidence handoff without adding new scope or treating the delivery claim as acceptance.

Leo: Client acceptance lead / Marta: Vendor delivery lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many criteria currently have verified evidence?

Answer and explanation

C. Four of six

Leo can verify four criteria; C5 and C6 lack evidence in the current record.

2. What follows from missing evidence for C5 and C6?

Answer and explanation

C. Their compliance has not yet been demonstrated in the record.

Missing proof leaves acceptance unresolved without establishing that the underlying functions fail.

3. Who may waive the criteria under the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

A. The appropriate authority, which Leo cannot replace alone

The brief explicitly says Leo cannot waive the criteria alone and records no waiver.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Four of six criteria have verified evidence. C5 and C6 are unverified in this record, not automatically failed or waived. A vendor completion claim and formal client acceptance are different statuses; neither substitutes for the other.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
statement of work
The agreed description of the vendor's work and deliverables, commonly SOW.refer to the statement of work
deliverable register
A record of promised outputs and their current status.update the deliverable register
acceptance plan
The arrangement for checking and deciding acceptance of outputs.confirm the acceptance plan
acceptance authority
The person or body empowered to accept the deliverable.identify the acceptance authority
acceptance evidence
Information demonstrating whether the agreed criteria are met.supply acceptance evidence
requirements matrix
A table linking requirements to identifiers, status, or supporting information.review the requirements matrix
evidence gap
Missing information needed to support a conclusion.identify an evidence gap
test result
The recorded outcome of a defined test.review a test result
test environment
The conditions and setup in which a test is performed.identify the test environment
test data
The inputs used in a defined test.specify the test data
expected result
The outcome required by the applicable test or criterion.compare with the expected result
actual result
The outcome observed when the defined test is performed.record the actual result
defect
A departure from a specified requirement or expected behavior.log a verified defect
nonconformance
Failure to meet an applicable specified requirement.document a nonconformance
punch list
A list of remaining items to address before a defined completion point.maintain a punch list
waiver
An authorized exception to a specified requirement under the governing rules.request a waiver
conditional acceptance
Acceptance limited by explicit unresolved conditions under the relevant authority.document conditional acceptance
formal acceptance
Recorded acceptance by the authorized party under the applicable process.confirm formal acceptance
handover package
The documents, outputs, and evidence transferred to the receiving team.complete the handover package
completion claim
A party's statement that its work is finished.verify a completion claim
commercial consequence
An effect on contractual or financial obligations requiring the governing terms.assess commercial consequences
contract owner
The person responsible for managing the relevant agreement.consult the contract owner
acceptance record
The documented acceptance decision and its basis or conditions.retain the acceptance record
evidence handoff
Transfer of identifiable supporting information to the reviewer.agree the evidence handoff

Request evidence against the agreed criteria, including the relevant test context, expected result, and actual result. Do not invent new requirements during acceptance. Payment or contractual remedies need the governing terms and proper authority, not an assumption from a status label.

C Notice the language

Be firm about evidence without escalating the tone

A vendor can mean development complete while the client means accepted against the agreed criteria. State the missing evidence and identifiers precisely. This keeps the discussion focused on the original agreement instead of implying new requirements, accusing someone of failure without proof, or approving an incomplete record.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the claim
I understand that your team considers development complete.
State the evidence position
We can verify four of the six agreed criteria.
Name the gaps
Evidence for C5 and C6 is missing from the handover package.
Avoid a false failure
Missing evidence does not itself establish a failed function.
Keep scope stable
We are asking for proof against existing criteria, not adding requirements.
Request traceability
Link each result to its criterion identifier and tested version.
Clarify the test context
Identify the environment, inputs, expected result, and actual result.
Separate the statuses
Delivered is not the same as formally accepted.
Keep the authority visible
I cannot waive these criteria on my own.
Avoid implied acceptance
A demonstration invitation is not an acceptance record.
Qualify conditional acceptance
Any conditional acceptance needs proper authority and explicit conditions.
Set the evidence deadline
Confirm when the missing evidence will be available for review.
Avoid premature closure
Keep the two criteria open until the evidence is assessed.
Handle terms correctly
Refer payment consequences to the contract owner and governing agreement.
Confirm the handoff
Name the sender, reviewer, file location, and next checkpoint.
Close with the actual state
Four verified, two awaiting evidence, acceptance not yet recorded.
6 language and precision notes
Complete
Name whose work stage and which completion definition.
Unverified
Not demonstrated; it does not automatically mean defective.
Failed
Requires evidence against the relevant criterion.
Waived
Requires an actual authorized exception.
Conditional
Keep the condition visible instead of shortening it to accepted.
Paid
A commercial status whose relation to acceptance depends on the agreement.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response preserves both accuracy and scope?

Answer and explanation

A. Please supply evidence for the existing C5 and C6 criteria; we are not adding new requirements.

The response requests the missing proof against the agreed scope without inventing failure or extra work.

2. A C5 screenshot comes from an earlier build. What is the appropriate response?

Answer and explanation

A. Check its applicability and traceability to the delivered version before treating C5 as demonstrated.

A result must be tied to the relevant criterion and version. An earlier result is neither automatically applicable evidence nor proof of a functional failure.

3. Which acceptance status is supported now?

Answer and explanation

A. No acceptance or waiver is recorded.

The brief explicitly leaves acceptance and waiver absent and limits Leo's authority.

4. What should be said about payment consequences?

Answer and explanation

A. They need assessment against the agreement by the appropriate owner.

The supplied facts do not establish the contractual payment rules or authorize a commercial conclusion.

E Conversations

Complete on the vendor side, not yet accepted

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Leo: Client acceptance lead / Marta: Vendor delivery lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • actual result
  • requirements matrix
  • waiver
  • conditional acceptance
  • acceptance record
  • contract owner
  • statement of work
  • completion claim
  • evidence gap
  • evidence handoff
  1. Marta
    Our delivery tracker says complete, but your sheet still has two open criteria. Is this a disagreement about the work or about the handover evidence?
  2. Leo
    It is a difference between your 1 and our acceptance status. Four of six criteria have verified support; C5 and C6 do not.
  3. Marta
    C5 is audit-log export and C6 is role-based access. I need to tell my team whether you found a failure or simply lack the results.
  4. Leo
    I am reporting an 2. We have not demonstrated those two criteria in this record; that is not the same as proving either function failed.
  5. Marta
    Understood. I was worried you had added requirements after delivery. Can we point the test lead back to the original acceptance criteria?
  6. Leo
    Yes, use the existing 3 and the agreed C5 and C6 identifiers. I am requesting support for those requirements, not a new feature.
  7. Marta
    I found an export screenshot, but it is from an earlier build. Would sending it let you close C5 on the delivered version?
  8. Leo
    Not without establishing its relevance. The 4 must link the criterion to the tested version and supporting result, so the reviewer can assess applicability.
  9. Marta
    I will ask for the setup, input data, and expected behavior as well. A screenshot labeled passed does not show what the test actually checked.
  10. Leo
    Include the 5 beside the expected result. For C6, make the tested role and relevant access behavior traceable to the agreed criterion.
  11. Marta
    If the evidence is not ready today, could you remove those two criteria from the sheet so our delivery record can close?
  12. Leo
    I cannot grant that 6 alone. Removing an agreed criterion is not a clerical correction; it needs the appropriate authority under the governing arrangement.
  13. Marta
    Could we request acceptance subject to later evidence? I am asking whether that is an option, not saying you have already accepted it.
  14. Leo
    Any 7 needs an authorized decision and explicit conditions. We have no such decision recorded, so the current acceptance status remains open.
  15. Marta
    I will send the evidence package or an itemized gap update by 14:00. Can you confirm who receives it and when you will check what arrived?
  16. Leo
    I will receive that 8 and check the package at 14:30. That is a review checkpoint, not a promise that the criteria will pass.
  17. Marta
    Our commercial team also asked whether they can invoice. I will not treat your agreement to review the package as a payment decision.
  18. Leo
    Refer invoicing to the 9 and the agreement. These facts do not establish the payment consequence, and I cannot invent it from a status label.
  19. Marta
    I will tell our team: four criteria verified, C5 and C6 awaiting evidence, package or gap update at 14:00, client check at 14:30. No waiver is recorded.
  20. Leo
    Confirmed. The 10 must capture the eventual authorized decision and its basis. Today we have agreed an evidence review, not acceptance of the deliverable.

Answers and explanations
  1. completion claim Turn 2. A completion claim is the vendor's statement of finished work, distinct from the client's acceptance evidence.
  2. evidence gap Turn 4. An evidence gap means the supporting information is missing, not that the function has been proven defective.
  3. statement of work Turn 6. The statement of work and agreed criteria define the original delivery scope, not new requirements invented during acceptance.
  4. requirements matrix Turn 8. The requirements matrix connects each criterion identifier with the relevant version, status, and supporting result.
  5. actual result Turn 10. The actual result is the recorded behavior observed in the identified test, to be compared with the expected result.
  6. waiver Turn 12. A waiver is an authorized exception; Leo does not have unilateral authority to remove the two criteria.
  7. conditional acceptance Turn 14. Conditional acceptance needs the relevant authority and explicit conditions; it is not established by discussing the option.
  8. evidence handoff Turn 16. The evidence handoff specifies who sends the missing results, where they are available, and when review can occur.
  9. contract owner Turn 18. The contract owner must assess commercial consequences against the governing agreement rather than a generic completion label.
  10. acceptance record Turn 20. The acceptance record should capture the actual authorized decision after evidence review, not convert delivery status into acceptance.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Marta: Our delivery tracker says complete, but your sheet still has two open criteria. Is this a disagreement about the work or about the handover evidence?
  2. Leo: It is a difference between your completion claim and our acceptance status. Four of six criteria have verified support; C5 and C6 do not.
  3. Marta: C5 is audit-log export and C6 is role-based access. I need to tell my team whether you found a failure or simply lack the results.
  4. Leo: I am reporting an evidence gap. We have not demonstrated those two criteria in this record; that is not the same as proving either function failed.
  5. Marta: Understood. I was worried you had added requirements after delivery. Can we point the test lead back to the original acceptance criteria?
  6. Leo: Yes, use the existing statement of work and the agreed C5 and C6 identifiers. I am requesting support for those requirements, not a new feature.
  7. Marta: I found an export screenshot, but it is from an earlier build. Would sending it let you close C5 on the delivered version?
  8. Leo: Not without establishing its relevance. The requirements matrix must link the criterion to the tested version and supporting result, so the reviewer can assess applicability.
  9. Marta: I will ask for the setup, input data, and expected behavior as well. A screenshot labeled passed does not show what the test actually checked.
  10. Leo: Include the actual result beside the expected result. For C6, make the tested role and relevant access behavior traceable to the agreed criterion.
  11. Marta: If the evidence is not ready today, could you remove those two criteria from the sheet so our delivery record can close?
  12. Leo: I cannot grant that waiver alone. Removing an agreed criterion is not a clerical correction; it needs the appropriate authority under the governing arrangement.
  13. Marta: Could we request acceptance subject to later evidence? I am asking whether that is an option, not saying you have already accepted it.
  14. Leo: Any conditional acceptance needs an authorized decision and explicit conditions. We have no such decision recorded, so the current acceptance status remains open.
  15. Marta: I will send the evidence package or an itemized gap update by 14:00. Can you confirm who receives it and when you will check what arrived?
  16. Leo: I will receive that evidence handoff and check the package at 14:30. That is a review checkpoint, not a promise that the criteria will pass.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Complete without acceptance evidence

Fictional vendor review using the lesson case: the vendor calls a deliverable complete, but evidence for two acceptance criteria cannot be found.

  1. Delivery manager: You marked the deliverable complete, but we cannot find evidence for two acceptance criteria.
  2. Vendor lead: Do you mean those checks failed, or that the results are missing from the handoff?
  3. Delivery manager: The results are missing. We aren't claiming failure; we need the evidence to assess acceptance.
  4. Vendor lead: Could you identify the criteria precisely so I can ask the right people for results?
  5. Delivery manager: I'll point to the two unlinked entries in the agreed acceptance checklist during our review.
  6. Vendor lead: Is tomorrow afternoon acceptable for checking whether those results exist and reporting any gaps?
  7. Delivery manager: Yes. Please provide the evidence or an explicit gap update then, rather than another completion label.
  8. Vendor lead: Should we discuss escalation now, or wait until we know what remains unresolved?
  9. Delivery manager: Let's first use that agreed checkpoint, then follow the project's escalation route for unresolved gaps.
  10. Vendor lead: Understood. I'll confirm receipt of the checklist and return with a criterion-by-criterion response.
Response time is not restoration time

Fictional support handoff: a draft summary confuses an acknowledged support ticket with restoration of the affected service; SLA wording has not been checked.

  1. Project coordinator: The vendor acknowledged our ticket within an hour. Can I mark the service issue resolved?
  2. Service manager: Acknowledgment confirms a response, not restoration. Do we have evidence the service works again?
  3. Project coordinator: Not yet. I also wrote that the vendor met the SLA, based on that response.
  4. Service manager: Please check which service-level agreement measure applies before making that claim about overall performance.
  5. Project coordinator: Could you send the relevant agreement section and identify who confirms service restoration?
  6. Service manager: I'll locate the section. The support team can report the service condition, but verification is pending.
  7. Project coordinator: Can we review both items at our scheduled afternoon handoff so the status is accurate?
  8. Service manager: Yes. Keep the acknowledgment time visible and label restoration as unconfirmed in the meantime.
  9. Project coordinator: I'll request a restoration update with supporting checks, not simply ask whether the ticket is closed.
  10. Service manager: That will give the receiving team a clear basis for deciding the next action.
Training files that the receiving team cannot open

Fictional vendor delivery: training videos were uploaded, but the customer training team reports access denied; no content review has occurred.

  1. Training manager: The video links return access denied, so our team hasn't been able to review the deliverable.
  2. Vendor coordinator: The upload completed yesterday. Does your message mean the videos themselves are unacceptable?
  3. Training manager: No. We haven't viewed them. Please arrange access for the named reviewers so assessment can begin.
  4. Vendor coordinator: Would making the folder public be the quickest way to solve the access problem?
  5. Training manager: We need the agreed restricted sharing arrangement, not public access to unpublished training materials.
  6. Vendor coordinator: Understood. Can you confirm the reviewer list through the project channel while I check permissions?
  7. Training manager: Yes. Please give us an access update before tomorrow's review session, or flag any delay earlier.
  8. Vendor coordinator: Once access works, will you confirm acceptance immediately so I can close our delivery task?
  9. Training manager: We'll confirm receipt first. Acceptance follows review against the agreed content and accessibility criteria.
  10. Vendor coordinator: I'll distinguish upload, accessible handoff, and acceptance in the delivery tracker from now on.

Unverified does not mean failed

Five criteria are agreed. Evidence supports four; the fifth has not been reviewed because its result is missing. No waiver or acceptance is recorded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: "Four criteria have supporting ___."

Answer and explanation

B. evidence

The briefing says four of the five criteria have demonstrated support.

2. Vendor: "The fifth remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unverified

Its result is missing, so compliance has not yet been demonstrated.

3. Client: "That does not itself prove a ___."

Answer and explanation

B. failure

Missing information is not evidence that the underlying function failed.

4. Vendor: "No acceptance has been ___."

Answer and explanation

C. recorded

The supplied facts explicitly leave the acceptance record absent.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Vendor and Cross-Functional Delivery
Original fictional case: Vendor lead Marta says a workflow deliverable is complete. Client acceptance lead Leo can verify four of six agreed criteria, but finds no evidence for C5, audit-log export, or C6, role-based access behavior. The missing evidence does not itself prove those functions fail. No acceptance or waiver is recorded, and Leo cannot waive criteria alone. Contractual payment consequences have not been assessed. Leo and Marta must agree the evidence handoff without adding new scope or treating the delivery claim as acceptance.
Language workshop: Complete on the vendor side, not yet accepted
Communication goal: Resolve a delivery-status disagreement by linking agreed criteria to evidence, distinguishing missing proof from failure, and naming the acceptance authority.
Language guidance: Four of six criteria have verified evidence. C5 and C6 are unverified in this record, not automatically failed or waived. A vendor completion claim and formal client acceptance are different statuses; neither substitutes for the other.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand that your team considers development complete. / We can verify four of the six agreed criteria. / Evidence for C5 and C6 is missing from the handover package. / Missing evidence does not itself establish a failed function. / We are asking for proof against existing criteria, not adding requirements. / Link each result to its criterion identifier and tested version. / Identify the environment, inputs, expected result, and actual result. / Delivered is not the same as formally accepted. / I cannot waive these criteria on my own. / A demonstration invitation is not an acceptance record. / Any conditional acceptance needs proper authority and explicit conditions. / Confirm when the missing evidence will be available for review. / Keep the two criteria open until the evidence is assessed. / Refer payment consequences to the contract owner and governing agreement. / Name the sender, reviewer, file location, and next checkpoint. / Four verified, two awaiting evidence, acceptance not yet recorded.
Vocabulary: statement of work: The agreed description of the vendor's work and deliverables, commonly SOW. / deliverable register: A record of promised outputs and their current status. / acceptance plan: The arrangement for checking and deciding acceptance of outputs. / acceptance authority: The person or body empowered to accept the deliverable. / acceptance evidence: Information demonstrating whether the agreed criteria are met. / requirements matrix: A table linking requirements to identifiers, status, or supporting information. / evidence gap: Missing information needed to support a conclusion. / test result: The recorded outcome of a defined test. / test environment: The conditions and setup in which a test is performed. / test data: The inputs used in a defined test. / expected result: The outcome required by the applicable test or criterion. / actual result: The outcome observed when the defined test is performed. / defect: A departure from a specified requirement or expected behavior. / nonconformance: Failure to meet an applicable specified requirement. / punch list: A list of remaining items to address before a defined completion point. / waiver: An authorized exception to a specified requirement under the governing rules. / conditional acceptance: Acceptance limited by explicit unresolved conditions under the relevant authority. / formal acceptance: Recorded acceptance by the authorized party under the applicable process. / handover package: The documents, outputs, and evidence transferred to the receiving team. / completion claim: A party's statement that its work is finished. / commercial consequence: An effect on contractual or financial obligations requiring the governing terms. / contract owner: The person responsible for managing the relevant agreement. / acceptance record: The documented acceptance decision and its basis or conditions. / evidence handoff: Transfer of identifiable supporting information to the reviewer.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.
