Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Dialogue 05: An honest status report
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Course: Project Management English

Dialogue 05: An honest status report
Original fictional setting: A sponsor questions a green status indicator.
Professional roles: Sponsor / Project manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Sponsor: Why is the project green when user testing hasn't started?
Project manager: You're right to question it. The report didn't reflect the new test-data delay.
Sponsor: What does that mean for the milestone?
Project manager: It's at risk. We haven't quantified the full effect yet.
Sponsor: Then tell the steering group what decision you need.
Project manager: I'll show the dependency, two recovery options, and the date we need an answer.
Sponsor: Make the reporting period explicit too.
Project manager: I'll correct the status and circulate the revised report before the meeting.
Vocabulary in this exchange: dependency: Work, approval, input, vendor, system, or decision that another activity relies on. / milestone: A significant planned point or achievement used to track progress.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 06: Resource negotiation
Original fictional setting: Two project managers need the same specialist.
Professional roles: Project manager / Resource manager
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Project manager: I need the security architect for five days next week.
Resource manager: She's already allocated to an incident review. Which tasks truly require her?
Project manager: The threat-model review and design signoff. Documentation could be prepared by our analyst.
Resource manager: Could two focused review sessions cover the immediate need?
Project manager: Possibly, if the analyst completes the evidence pack first.
Resource manager: I'll ask the architect to assess that plan before changing her allocation.
Project manager: I'll send the decisions and preparation required for each session.
Resource manager: Then we can confirm a feasible commitment and flag any remaining gap.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 07: Handover to operations
Original fictional setting: The delivery team prepares to transfer a service.
Professional roles: Delivery manager / Operations lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Delivery manager: The build is complete. Are you ready to accept the service?
Operations lead: Not yet. The runbook lacks escalation contacts, and support training is incomplete.
Delivery manager: Let's distinguish technical completion from operational acceptance.
Operations lead: Exactly. We also need the outstanding defect list and monitoring arrangements.
Delivery manager: I'll assign owners and dates to those handover items.
Operations lead: I'll review the completed evidence against our acceptance checklist.
Delivery manager: Can we schedule the acceptance meeting provisionally?
Operations lead: Yes, provided the invitation makes clear that approval is still pending.
Vocabulary in this exchange: escalation: Raising an issue to a higher authority or different function because risk, urgency, or decision rights require it.
END REFERENCE

Dialogue 08: Lessons learned
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Project Management English
Professional audience: project managers, program managers, PMO staff, scrum masters, delivery leads, operations managers, and cross-functional coordinators
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not a project-management certification, contract interpretation, engineering instruction, or release authorization. All organizations, dates, numbers, and decisions are invented. Actual work must follow the governing agreement, approved project controls, applicable rules, and responsible decision makers.
Dialogue 08: Lessons learned
Original fictional setting: A project team reviews a missed milestone.
Professional roles: Project manager / Workstream lead
Published dialogue (reference script, not my own performance):
Project manager: What contributed to the missed milestone beyond the supplier delay?
Workstream lead: We didn't verify the dependency early enough, and the escalation route was unclear.
Project manager: What change would help the next project?
Workstream lead: A dependency review at kickoff with named owners and trigger dates.
Project manager: Who can put that into the standard planning checklist?
Workstream lead: The delivery office. I'll draft the addition for its review.
Project manager: Let's record the action and a check that it was adopted.
Workstream lead: Agreed. A lesson isn't useful if it stays only in these minutes.
Vocabulary in this exchange: dependency: Work, approval, input, vendor, system, or decision that another activity relies on. / milestone: A significant planned point or achievement used to track progress. / escalation: Raising an issue to a higher authority or different function because risk, urgency, or decision rights require it.
END REFERENCE
Create more scenario dialogues — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Extend the conversation library
1. Propose six distinct, common scenarios in this field beyond the supplied case or script. For each, name the setting, two or more professional roles, the communication problem and one useful terminology focus. Include routine coordination, clarification, a complication, disagreement, handoff and follow-up where relevant. Choose scenarios that fit this profession, rather than forcing unsuitable situations into it.
2. Ask me to choose one scenario, or request all six in sequence. Stop and wait. Do not write all the scripts before I choose.
3. For the selected scenario, write an original fictional dialogue of 12-18 substantial speaking turns between two or three professionals. Name each role, establish an actual work problem, and let the exchange progress through questions, clarification, competing constraints and a credible next step or explicitly unresolved issue. Use the occupation's natural nomenclature and register. Avoid an interview between a teacher and a learner, generic small talk, and inserting a glossary definition into every reply. Explain specialist terms outside the dialogue instead.
4. After the script, explain five useful expressions in context and identify two grammar or register choices. Give three four-choice questions about the speakers' reasoning, with exactly one answer supported by the dialogue. Wait for my selections, then explain why each answer fits and why its alternatives fail. Add a role-switch reading using the supplied script; do not ask for an essay or invented written reply.
5. Before presenting a script, check names, numbers, chronology, roles and terminology for consistency. Label invented facts and acknowledge uncertain specialist usage. If I requested all six, deliver one complete script at a time and wait for "next". Make each scenario materially different.

Read your ready-to-use prompt 
Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions after the REFERENCE block. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Project Management English
Professional audience: project managers, program managers, PMO staff, scrum masters, delivery leads, operations managers, and cross-functional coordinators
Scope: Original fictional language practice, not a project-management certification, contract interpretation, engineering instruction, or release authorization. All organizations, dates, numbers, and decisions are invented. Actual work must follow the governing agreement, approved project controls, applicable rules, and responsible decision makers.
Lesson 01: Project Charter and Scope Definition
Original fictional case: Sponsor Greg requests a dashboard in four weeks, but the initial brief does not identify users, decisions, or exclusions. In a planning meeting with project manager Elena, he proposes a first release for operations leads doing weekly queue triage: overdue-item visibility and an export. Automated work allocation would be excluded. Data access and acceptance criteria remain unconfirmed, and no scope baseline is approved. Elena must turn the request into a reviewable definition without promising the four-week target.
Language workshop: A dashboard is not yet a defined project
Communication goal: Clarify the users, decisions, boundaries, and acceptance basis behind a sponsor request before treating a target date as a commitment.
Language guidance: A dashboard is an output; improved queue decisions would be an intended outcome. The proposed users and exclusions narrow the request, but they do not confirm access, acceptance, or schedule feasibility. Four weeks remains a target until supported and authorized.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which decision should the dashboard help users make? / The proposed first users are operations leads. / They will use it for weekly queue triage. / The first release would show overdue items and provide an export. / Automated allocation is outside the proposed first release. / A delivered screen is not itself evidence of better queue decisions. / Four weeks is the requested target, not yet a delivery commitment. / We still need to confirm access to the source data. / What evidence will show that the first release meets the agreed need? / Who is authorized to accept the defined output? / Record the data-access assumption until it is verified. / A useful future feature is not automatically part of this release. / Replace looks good with a checkable acceptance condition. / These are proposed boundaries pending review. / Link the requirements to the scope and acceptance evidence. / Confirm users, decision use, inclusions, exclusions, and approval route.
Vocabulary: project charter: A document formally establishing a project and its high-level authority and purpose. / project sponsor: The person accountable for sponsorship and relevant business decisions. / business need: The problem or opportunity the work is intended to address. / business case: The justification for the proposed investment and expected value. / project objective: A specific result the project is intended to achieve. / deliverable: A defined output to be produced by the project. / outcome: A change or result enabled by using the project's outputs. / benefit: A measurable improvement of value from the change. / scope statement: A description of the work and boundaries included in the project. / scope baseline: The approved scope reference used to manage and assess changes. / in scope: Included within the defined project boundary. / out of scope: Excluded from the defined project boundary. / assumption: A premise used for planning that needs appropriate validation or monitoring. / constraint: A limit within which the project must operate. / requirement: A defined need or condition the solution must satisfy. / acceptance criterion: A condition used to decide whether an output is acceptable. / stakeholder: A person or group affected by or able to affect the project. / end user: A person who will use the delivered capability. / decision use: The decision or action a delivered information product supports. / work breakdown structure: A hierarchical division of project work into manageable components, abbreviated WBS. / work package: A defined unit of work that can be assigned and managed. / requirements traceability: Links connecting needs, requirements, work, and evidence. / success measure: A defined indicator used to assess the intended result. / scope approval: Authorization of the stated project boundaries and work.
Speaking task: Read the corrected charter dialogue. Name operations leads, weekly triage, overdue-item visibility, and the export before stating the excluded automation. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the four-week target and data-access assumption separate from approval. Complete and check the transfer. Read the proposed export boundary without adding automated allocation or a delivery commitment.
Partner: Greg: Project sponsor / Elena: Project manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A proposed first release includes a weekly export for operations leads. Automated allocation is excluded. Data access is unconfirmed, and the target date has not been approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Greg: We should keep the requirements connected to the reason for the project. Otherwise an attractive feature may distract us from the weekly triage need.
Elena: Use requirements traceability to preserve those connections. The record should show why the work exists, how it is checked, and whether it belongs in this release.
Greg: Good. Please bring the definition and unresolved decisions to the review. I will not announce the date or the wider automation feature as approved today.
Elena: Then we can seek scope approval on a clear basis. The immediate result is a reviewable proposal, not a promise built from a product name and a target alone.
END REFERENCE

AI responses can contain mistakes. Compare feedback with the lesson and check specialist claims against the course references. Use these exercises for language practice; follow qualified guidance for actual professional decisions.

For teachers and study partners

Ask learners to respond before revealing the model. Give feedback on one meaning issue and one language pattern, then let them repeat. For mixed levels, offer the frames first and remove them in the second round.

Language notes and further reading

The cases and explanations are original teaching material. The references provide language frameworks and selected professional context; use current local guidance for actual work.

  • Piney, C. Integrated Project Risk and Issue Management. Project Management Institute, 2012.
  • UK Government Project Delivery. Government Functional Standard GovS 002: Project Delivery.
  • NASA. Systems Engineering Handbook, Appendix D: Requirements Verification Matrix.
